. ſtand excuſed for printing this Skefch 


of bis Caſe, his Misfortunes having deprived 
him of the Power of keeping a Clerk, or. 


paying for the tranſcribing Copies, which 
has induced him reluctantly to take this 


Method, not by way of diffuſing them more 


widely, but of procuring the few he means 
to preſent to ſome ſele& and worthy Charac- 
ters at the leaſt Expence poſſible. To thoſe 


he chearfully appeals, not only with due De- 


ference, but alſo with the Confidence natu- 
rally ariſing from the Mens conſcia recti; and 


from their Candour and Goodneſs he draws 


the pleaſing Hope (notwithſtanding the 
Weight of his Oppreſſions has nearly over- 


' whelmed him) that he may yet live to meet 
with JUSTICE ; without which, his laſt 


melancholy Reſource muſt be to preſent to

SKETCH of the CASE, Ge. 


R. ROBERT KIRKE, late Conſul at Algiers, having ſuffered 

more than language can well expreſs, and being driven to the 

greateſt neceſſity with a large family, ruined by the moſt unheard of and 

| ſhocking cruelties and miſrepreſentations, deprived of the conſtituti- 
onal right of an Engliſhman, namely a proper hearing in his own de- 
fence for upwards of 13 years, though he has made every effort in his 
power during that time to obtain it“; notwithſtanding he was not only 

| or- 


That Mr. Kirke has not been treated with the Candour and Lenity uſually ſhewn to 
the Servants of the Crown , in Caſes of Complaint made againſt them, is evident, from the 
cloſe of the following letter from the Secretaty of State's Office, viz. 

Copy of Letter from Robert Wood, Eſq; ** Secretary of State under Lord 
| Weymouth, to Mr, Welford.

together with your lettey of the 21ſt paſt to me on the ſame ſubject, his Majeſty has order- 
ed ſtrict inquiry to be made into the truth of the allegations of the petition, and of the let- 
ter, and that according to the reſult of ſuch inquiry, his Majeſty will give ſuch further ore 
ders as ſhall appear neceſſary, in order to facilitate the means of JvsTICE to both parties

10 William Egerton, 818 


DEAR SIR, 8 
WERE I to give you a juſt idea of che hardſhip and cru- 


elty of my ſufferings, it would be neceſſary to relate the hiſtory of 


my long and faithful ſervice from the time of entering into the Royal 
Navy in 1745, to my unjuſt and fatal diſmiſſion from the office of Agent 
and Conſul General at Algiers; but this would be giving you the 
trouble of reading a long and diſagreeable narrative; I ſhall therefore 
confine myſelf to thoſe facts which gave riſe to that cataſtrophe, ſo 
ruinous to myſelf and family, and refer to the incloſed memorial 1. 

In the year 1764, I was very happily and comfortably ſettled in a 


— Little retreat at Buſby, having, during a ſervice of nineteen years, with 


great care and induſtry, ſaved as much as brought me an annual income 
of upwards of four hundred pounds; I was, beſides, Purſer of the Nep- 
tune, a ninety gun ſhip then in ordinary at Portſmouth, which, toge- 


ther with a little buſineſs I tranſacted, made me a clear income of about 
ſeven hundred per annum. When the late Earl of Halifax, in whoſe 


neighbourhood I lived, and with whom J had the honour to be very in- 


timate, politely made me the oifer of the Conſulſhip of Algiers, ſaying, 


He had long wiſhed to ſerve me, and ſet the good little woman (mean-

an opportunity of making his defence, Lord Weymouth alſo direfs me to acquaint you, 
that, as on the one hand the King wilt not alle his commiſſion to protect any man againft

ing my wife) down happy. I replied, I was much obliged to his Lord- 
ip; but confiding in his experienced freindſhip, and the familiarity 
he had always indulged me with, ventured to make ſome objections I 
had to the place; which he anſwered: with his uſual good nature, and 
added that I ought to conſider it was fix hundred a year, paid by the 
Treaſury. He further ſaid, "the Miniſters could never get any certaim 
or ſatisfactory account of the Barbary States, which were eternally teaſ- 
ing this country ; and as he knew me to be a man he could confide in, 
wiſhed me to go: and that his Lordſhip only intended the appointment 
as a foundation for my future advancement. I begged a few days to 
conſider of it; he replied, let me know as ſoon as you can, ſor Mini- 
ſters are ſtrangely teaſed when a vacancy happens, till they can ſay it is 
filled up. I bewed, and promiſed expedition; and the next. morning 
went to town, and conſulted my friend William Sharpe, Eſq; firft 
Clerk of the Privy Council, by whoſe advice J returned to his Lordſhip, 
and told him, I ſhould be happy to receive his Majeſty's commands 
when prepared; and immediately, on my name's being announced in 
the Gazette as Conſul to Algiers, another perſon was appointed Purſer 
of the Neptune in my ſtead, I then equip'd myſelf and family for that 
expedition, at an expence of near three thouſand pounds, looking vpon 
it I was fixed for life, promotion only excepted, and ſometime after- 
wards: ſet out, giving up my buſineſs, &c. 

On my artival at Algiers, I found the two Houſes ceciaiy deeuphuls 
by the Britiſh Conſuls in great diſorder, and very much out of repair, 
the perſon I ſucceeded being a ſingle gentleman; it therefore became 
neceſſary to employ a number of Workmen, at a confiderable expence, 
to make them habitable ; five days after the finiſhing of which I was 
ordered home, to anſwer in perſon an imaginary complaint of Thomas 
Harriſon, Eſq; then Commander of the ſquadron in the Mediterranean, 
who had miſtaken the treaty with the Barbary States, by not only en- 
deavouring to fill the Centurion, one of the King's Ships (on board of 
which his broad pendant was flying) with Corn and Barley, but alſo in - 
fiſting on the Engliſh having liberty to export the ſame from the Port of 
Algiers, from which, all the miſchief that afterwards enſued aroſe, 
being a thing contrary in the firſt place to the Commodores infrudions, 
and what is ſtill clearer to the expreſs law of the Algerines, and which the 
Dey had not power to grant even to his own ſubjecli, as they take an 

by ET oath 


s SKETCH OF THE CASE, G. 
oarh on their appointment not to permit it; but the Commodore weakly 
Imagining it was in my power, as Engliſh Conſul, to obtain a comple- 


tion of what his people had been illicitiy employed about for near three 


days, miſtaking, as I ſaid before, our treaty with that State, particularly 
the tenth Article, as I could no more have obtained the Commodore's 
demands (had I been made acquainted with them) than the F rench 
Ambaſſador could the exportation of wool from hence, or our ſhips im- 
port tobacco unnoticed into Spain. Notwithſtanding which the Com- 
modore was cruel enough to write a wicked and fallacious letter of com- 
plaint againſt me, not only to the then Secretary of State and Lords of 
the Admiralty, but alſo „ the late Duke of Vork. The conſequence 
was, his Highneſs privatcly applied to Lord Egmont, firſt Lord of the 
Admiralty, who influenced the board; and they, as a board, applied 
to the Duke of Richmond, Secretary of State for my total Diſmiſſion. 
The Duke candidly replied, that though he did not know Mr. Kirke, 
or any of his connections, he could not think of doing ſo unjuſt a thing, 
as to diſmiſs any man azheard, but as the board of Admiralty made a 
point of it, he would order me home to anſwer the complaint, which he 
did, and fent out a Mr. Sampſon to officiate in my abſence. | 

My Friend, Mr. Sharpe, repeatedly urged his many years knowledge 
of me, and that he was certain I could not be guilty of the charges ſet 
forth by Commodore Harriſon, and wiſhed I might (as is uſual) have a 
copy of the complaint to anſwer before I was ordered home, To which 
his Grace replied, he could do no leſs than ſend for me, as the Admi- 
ralty had officially demanded my total Di/miſjon ; nevertheleſs, if I was 
able to anſwer the complaint, his Grace would move the King to ſend 
me back with honour to my employment, and pay my expences amply. 
TI received his Grace's letter, and obeyed the contents, leaving great 
part of my houſehold furniture and other effects behind me, for which I 
have not as yet had any ſatisfaction. And whilſt I was performing qua- 
rantine in Gibraltar Bay, I wrote a letter to Lord Shelburne, who ſuc- 
ceeded the Duke of Richmond as Secretary of State, declaring my inno- 
cence; notwithſtanding which, I and my family were obliged to perform 
a ſecond quarantine in Stangate Creek, whilſt Mr. Fraſer, who had been 
appointed Con/ul in my ſtead, ſet out for Algiers. After all this was 
done, and I had fully and fatisfaQorily juſtified my conduct, Lord Shel- 


da (as you were ſo kind to tell me) accoſted you at the Maſquerade 
£ ; made

ever ſince; and am now worn out by age and diſappointment, with a 
numerous family, deprived of the common conveniences and neceſſaries 
of life, which muſt ſhortly end in our total deſtruction. The only apo- 
jogy I can make for being thus troubleſome is, the knowledge I have of 
your humanity, and a thorough conviction within myſelf, not only of 
your readineſs to ſerve your friends 1 in general,” but particularly, 
Dear SIR, *

To the now King of England, beland. Scotland, and France, King ; 
and High Monarch, the moſt beloved of all the world, this humble and 
lovely paper. 

WE, the moſt illuſtrious Mahomet Paſhaw, Dey of che famous and 

warlike City and Kingdom of Algiers,” in Barbary, do, in juſtice to, 
and from the real eſteem we now and always bore Robert Kirke, Eſa; | 
his Britannic Majeſty's Agent and Conſul General, who has, with ſo 
much Honour and integrity reſided with us for ſome time paſt, ſignify 
and declare to the moſt high, powerful, and mighty King, George the 

Third, King of England and all thoſe ſeas; -King-of-Scottand; King of 
France, and King of Ireland, Defender of the Chriſtian Faith, and 
King of. Kings, our ſteady and conſtant F tiend, and good Ally, u that from, 
the firſt entry of the ſaid Robert Kirke i in this our 'wailike City, and 
during all the time of his continuance with us, he, h has aged with pro- 
priety and integrity; that his mild and prudent conduct has ever 
rendered his perſon and memory; not only an ornament to his own, 
country, but the delight of ours; that his ſteady and unbiaſſed actions, 
have given us the higheſt proofs of his fidelity; that he. has ever ſtood 
forth in the ſtrongeſt manner for the honor and intereſt of his own nation, 
without infringing the laws and euſtoms of ours; that he has hot only 
been attentive to protect, but alſo to promotes? trade and commerce, 
which has flouriſhed much ſince his reſidence here: So praying God to 
bleſs and continue our faſt Friend and powerful Ally in his throne for 
ever, not doubting but that his faithful Conſul, Robert Kirke, Efqz 
will meet that favour in his Sovereign's preſence, that from his conduct 
and merit, whilſt with us, he may deſervedly hope for. 5 To all which 
we have cauſed our Royal Seal to be affixed, as, alſo thoſe of the Lords, 

or Governors in power, in teſtimony of our general approbation and 


good will towards him, hoping to ſee him here again as, Canſul; and 
that this our recommendation and certificate may have its due-weight 


in the Conſul's favour, when delivered to our high _ powerful ul Friend;

* This Circumſtance was the origin of Commodore Harriſon's diſguſt, for (as he told 
Conſul Kirke, on his coming firſi from Mahon to Algiers) it had been always cuſtomary 
to give or ſend the commander of the ſquadron in the Mediterranean the commiſſion of am- 
daſſador to the States of Barbary, The fact was thus: The late Earl of Briſtol, then the 
Hon, Capt. Harvey, was appointed to the Command of the Centurion, and was to have 
taken the late Duke of York en board, and carried him to the Mediterranean, where Capt. 
Harvey was to have commanded the ſquadron. On this occaſion he expended between tuo 
and three thouſand pounds in making the ſhip comm. dious, purchaſing velvet hangings for 
the cabbin, ſtate room, &c. together with the large quantity of wines, ſtock, and various 
other necefſaries he laid in for that purpoſe z ſoon after which, Capt. Harvey was appointed 
a Gentleman of the Bedchamber to his Majeſty, and brought into Parliament, On which 
2 proper perſon was to be found to take the command, carry the Duke, and reimburſe 


Capt. Harvey the large ſum he had expended on his out-fic; when none could be found but 


Capt. Harriſon, who was a very young poſt captain, but his father having left him a very 
large fortune, he (for the ſake of ſo high an appointment) paid Capt. Harvey the ſum he 
had expended, took the command, and failed accordingly with his Royal Highneſs on 
board for the Mediterranean. Some time after which, Capt. Clevland, fon of Mr. Secre-

REPORT. 


In April, 1966, Commodore Harriſon, then Commander in Chief 
in the Mediterranean, went with part of his ſquadron to Algiers, and 
during his ſtay there, a very diſagreeable miſunderſtanding happened 
between him and Mr. Kirke. The Commodore complained to the then 
Lords of the Admiralty of Mr. Kirke's behaviour, and th:ir Lordſhips 
communicated to his Grace the Duke of Richmond, at that time Secre- 
tary of State, the particulars of the Commodore's complaints, and they 
were ſocn afterwards tranſmitted to the Duke of Richmond direaly by 
the Commodore, as appears in his Grace's letter to Mr, Fs * 


the 28th July, 1766.

As it was determined Conſul Kirke ſhould come kome to "anſwer the

will be found, that Mr. Kirke's words were exactly as follows, viz. 
* an unfortunate miſunderſlanding which . bappened wwhilft Commo- 
dore Harriſon was bere in the Centurion 1. And the Dey's letter, which 

accompanied Conſul Kirke's, with thoſe expreſſions in it, moſt fully 
and clearly ſets forth, that it was owing to Commodore Harriſon's 
weakneſs, and miſconceiving the Treaty, that firſt occaſioned the miſ- 
underſtanding with the late as well as preſent Dey; and as a confirma- 
tion of the truth of what Mr. Kirke now aflerts, that no miſunderſtand- 
ing was ſubſiſting between him and the Commodore, it will moſt clearly 
appear by the Dey's letter of the 2zoth May, 1766+, to his Majeſty, 
which accompanied Mr. Kirke's of the 22d of the ſame month, that a 
very exact and minute detail of the miſunderſtanding alluded to is given 
by the Dey, and plainly ſhews, that it was between the Commodore 
and the late as well as preſent Dey, and not between the Commodore 
and Conſul Kirke; and that it firſt aroſe in November, -1765, and not 
in April 1766; and in that letter the Dey not only clearly ſhews that 
Conſul Kirke took no part whatever, or was in any degree concerned in

* utmeft endeaucurs in his nation's behalf.” And as a further confirmation

vhoſe good Opinion, as he has ever endeavoured to merit, he is ſtill am- 

itious to preſerve. .

Letter 1. the Dey of Algiers 2 His Britannic Majeſly 
dated 20th May, 1766. 


WITH the help of God Almighty. The King of our time, King 
the Turks and Moors, Friend of the ſtrong people, Defender of the ju 


Mahomet Baſhaw his ſervant, Dey of Algiers, to the King of England

my heart, Poſſeſſor of crowns; I wiſh that this letter may meet you i Mh! 
perfect health, and that God Almighty may enlarge the days of you aw 
Majeſty's life, and keep them always delighted in heavenly muſic, and * 


all the proſperity this world can afford. Amen. 

With the help, and under the protection of Jeſus the ſon of Mary Ane

deceaſed Ally Baſhaw, in which time I was Haſtfagee, or 'Treaſurer, 
we had heard and know that your Majeſty's Conſul, Robert Kirke, no 
reſiding with us, was always acting according to the proper and righ

all the friendſhip in his power, not only for your Majeſty's fake, but for 
your Conſul's alſo; and whatever he deſired to have for the uſe of th: 
ſhips of his nation, whether men of war, or Merchant men, ſaid All: 
Baſhaw allow'd him without reſtriction, having freed the port of thi

The Conſul then went off to pay his compliments to the Commodore. 
nd defiring to know his commands: the Commodore replied, I ſhould 
now them by and by; and that he wanted to ſee the Dey, and ſhould 
ome on ſhore, about half an hour after two o'clock, for that purpoſe. 
Del told him, a little before two, if he pleaſed, was the beſt and moſt 
onvenient ; but whatever hour he choſe to fix, I would ſend to let the 
ey know, and doubted not but he might ſee him at any time. The 
ommodore replied, he would be on ſhore between one and two. I then 
ook leave, and returned in the Captain of the port's boat, when the 
enturion ſaluted as uſual. „ 

At half after one, I went down to the Marine to meet the Commodore, 
ho, on coming on ſhore, was ſaluted with five guns, which the Cen- 
urion returned. He brought with him two other Captains, and a great 
he umber of Officers, who drank coffee, and eat ſweetmeats, with the Ad- 
miral at the marine. I then told the Commodore, I ſhould be glad to 

now his commands with the Dey, if he thought proper ; and that I 
looked upon it as neceſſary to acquaint him with all that had been done 
,Welative to Benzerzo Rais, who brought in his Majeſty's Store Shi p, the 
Florida, The Commodore then replicd, that what he came about was 
no buſineſs of mine: I-ſhould hear that preſently. I then told him every 
particular mentioned in my letter of the 21ſt of April laſt, to the Secretary 
of State ft, in regard to Benzerzo's diſgrace, &c. in converſation, as we 


pliments being over, the Commodore acquainted the Dey he was /ent by 


apaper, he delivered it to a Jew he brought with him from Mahon, and 
ordered him to read it to the Dey in a language the Dey underſtood, 
ſaying that that was the contents of what he came upon. The Conſul 
then ſaid, ſeeing a paper ſtrangely ſcribbled on,, that if the Commodore 
pleaſed, he ſhould be glad to know what that paper was; but the Com- 
modore replied that was not my buſineſs, and that he ſuppoſed I knew 


not that he had particular Orders from his King : to which the Conſul 


replied, indeed he did not; but if that was the caſe, It was very well.

24 APPENDIX. 


The Jew, having the paper in his hands (wrote on in Hebrew charaQer, 
but Spaniſh language) deſired to know of the Dey in what language he 
would have it explained to him, whether in Italian or Arabic; to whit 
the Dey replied, in what language he would (the Dey's own Drugoma 
attending). The Jew then read the paper in the Spaniſh languare 
the contents of which were, as near as my interpreters could inform me, 
as follows, viz. 

„The King, my Se, ſent me here, 1 in order to demand ſatisfaRion 
< for the inſolent behaviour of Rais Mahomet Benzerzo, Captain cf the 
< privateer, who brought into Algiers his Majeſty's Store-Ship Florida; 


Land that ſaid cruiſer had afterwards met a ſchooner, bound from Boſton 


*:20 Liſbon, i into which he had fired ſeveral muſket ſhot, and likewiſe took 
out of her ſome ſalt fiſh, and other things: and that he, the Commo. 
„ dore, was ready to ſend a man of war to Liſbon, to bring him a particular 
* account of the damages ſhe had ſuſtained; and that he required of the Dey 
« ſatisfaction for the ſame. All which hedemanded in the Kin g his maſter'; 
„name: and likewiſe, that orders ſhould be forthwith given to all his erui. 
< ſers never to commit the like irregularities for the future.” To which the 


Dey anſwered, that as for Benzerzo's behaviour, he had already, upon 


the Conſul's application, not only diſmantled his ſhip, but likewiſe pub- 
lickly broke him at the Marine, and rendered him incapable of ſerving 
the State any more, by taking from him the Conſul's paſſports, &c. 
which he directly ſent to the Conſul's houſe : and as to his firing upon 
the ſchooner, it was ever known to be the cuſtom of all cruiſers to fire at 
any veſſels they meet at ſea, when they will not bring to, after firing a 
gun without ball, afterwards making a ſmoke; and if that would not 
bring them to, then to fire ſhot, which was the preſent caſe : but that 
the Dey would make Benzerzo pay any damage the ſchooner had ſuſ. 
tained, ſo ſoon as the particulars came to the Conſul's hands: and that 
the ſtricteſt orders had (as the Conſul knew already) been iſſued, and 


ſhould be again repeated, to all his cruiſers, never to commit any the 


feaſt irregularity whatever towards the Engliſh, as they were, and al- 
ways had been, good friends. The Commodore then ſaid he had Order. 


from his King to come on this affair, and that he was obliged to deliver 


his meſſage ; and that he purpoſed ſailing the next morning, if the wind 
was fair; and that he took that opportunity of taking leave, 'The Dey's 


Drugoman then ſaid, that he had ordered ſome refreſhments for the | 
King's ſhips, as cuſtomary: but the Commodore's anſwer was, that he 


was very much obliged to the Dey, but that he wanted nothing, The 


Jew that came with the Commodore, then alked leave, in the Commo-

e Conſul, which the Conſul returned, and went directly home, the 
nſul's Drugoman attending the Commodore,

6; SIR, Mahon, September 13th, 1765. 
THE magiſtrates of this place having preſented a memorial to 
ie, defiring that I would again apply to the Dey of Algiers for his per- 
Wilton to the Mahoneſe to buy Corn in his Dominions, I have wrote to 
te Dey, and ſent him the Magiſtrates Memorial. 

Mr. Bruce put in here by a contrary wind, and gave us very little 
opes of our getting any Cox from the Dey, for that though the Dey 
as very well diſpoſed towards the Engliſh, yet the exportation of Cor x 
25 notoriouſly known to be monopolized by the Dey's favorites, and 
ren ſent by them to Cadiz, and other ports of his enemies. 

Mr. Cleveland wrote me, that the Dey had promiſed him this Iſland 
ould never want Corn while he had any in his country; 1t is therefore 
os matter of ſome ſurprize, that the veſſels which I have ſent over on 
„a rpoſe for Corn, ſhould have returned without any; and that Mr. 
leveland has not acquainted me with the reaſons for refuſing them to 
ad, after having led me to expect, that permiſſion would be granted 
br all ſuch veſſels to load as brought Certificates from me. 

I have acquainted the Secretary of State with my application to the 
ey, and to Mr, Cleveland, as likewiſe with the purport of Mr. Cleve- 
and's letter to me, that no imputation of negle& might fall upon re,

ple here know full well, and look on it as immediately for the ſervice of 
the King of Great Britain's Troops at Mahon, if otherwiſe, that I reap a 
conſiderable advantage therefrom, or that I ſhould not be ſo urgent in 
the matter. | 3 

The bearer, Captain Oliver, pretended to have had your ſpecial 
order to deliver your letter to the Dey unizown to me; which, if he 
found not ſufficient to procure him his loading of Corn, then he was to 
deliver me the letter you wrote me on bit Majeſty's Service: this, Cap- 
tain Oliver aſſerted; which I own I cannot conceive to be true, as it 
would appear exceſſive ſtrange, nay even injuriqus to me, that it ſhould 
be left to the option of a Mahoneſe Patron, to deliver or not, at his plea- 
ſare, a letter wrote to me on the King's immediate Serwice, by the Com- 
mander in Chief of one of his garriſons, which makes it abſolutely ne- 
ceſſary that you ſhould be made acquainted therewith. | 

J muſt beg leave further to obſerve to you, that the Dey, upon dif- 
ferent occaſions, gave Mr, Cleveland and me the firongelt aſſurances, 
that no other informations or meſſages whatever regarding the affairs of 
Great Britain, ſhould be received or relied on, but thoſe that came 
through my Hands, as the proper Channel; and that no meaſures relative 
thereto ſhould be taken, without conſulting me previouſly ; which, I 
ſuppoſe, occaſicned the Dey's ſending the tiſcary or licenſe, directly 
to my houſe.

| Algiers, 28th February, 1766. 

SOME time before your arrival, when Colonel Townſhend 
commanded at Mahon, he was pleaſed to write me a letter, dated 13th 
September laſt; in anſwer to which, I wrote him two letters; the one 
dated the 4th, and the other the gth October following : but as he 
thought proper to anſwer only that of the 4th, and never took the leaſt 
notice whatever of that of the gth, which was by far the moſt material, 
I am under the neceſſity of troubling you, Sir, with a duplicate, under 
a flying ſeal, and requeſt the favor, after peruſal, that you will be 
pleaſed to cauſe it to be delivered into Mr, Townſhend's hands, and uſe 
your influence to procure me an anſwer thereto, as Mr, Townfhiend's 
letter to which mine is an anſwer was directed to me on the King's Ser- 
vice. I hope you will excuſe the trouble I give you on ae occaſion, 
and believe me to be, with great regand, 


SIR,

Your moſt obedient and moſt humble ſervant, 
Lieut. Gov. Jchnſton, Mahon. ROBERT KIRKE.

MY LORD, Algiers, 31ſt October, 1766. 

I was honoured with your Lordſhip's letter of the 1 Auguſt on 
he 18th inſtant, by the Brig Nancy, David Maſterton, Commander, 
om Leghorn, and I beg my moſt humble congratulations upon the 
mtents, and thanks for the information your Lordſhip was pleaſed to 
ive me in the poſtſcript thereto of the alterations lately made; but I 
nnot avoid expreſſing my great concern and ſurprize, at the direction 
n your Lordſhip's letter, finding thereby, as alſo by common report, 
at I am to come home to anſwer ſome complaints, made againſt me by 


ommodore Harriſon when Commander in Chief of the Squadron in 
eſe Seas; but as nothing of the kind has yet been ſignified to me, I 
all content myſelf by aſſuring your Lordſhip of my intire innocence in 
gard to the cauſe of any complaints that can have been fabricated againſt 
ie, and that I ſhall with great chearfulneſs and alacrity obey ſuch com- 
nds as I may have the honour to receive for my future government, 
oping to be able to juſtify myſelf in every particular. S 
I am informed by Rear Admiral Vandergen, Commander of one of 
2 States-Generals Frigates, called the Caſtor, of 36 guns, who arrived 
ere with ſome preſents for this State, that he left Commodore Spry at 
lbraltar, who had been very ill there, but was got much better, and 
ding to Morocco, from whence he was to. proceed to this place, with 
ne Mr. Sampſon to act as Conſul; and as I can make no doubt of the 
uth of the Admiral's aſſertion, I take this earlieſt opportunity by his 
ip to forward this letter, to acquaint your Lordſhip, that all things 
re remain in the moſt perfect Rate of tranquility ; and that I ſhall give 
ery information and inſtruction in my power to Mr. Sampſon, or who- 
er elſe may be appointed to conduct his Majeſty's ſervice in this Coun-

Mr. Kirke has been informed, it is an invariable rule, whenever any Complaint is 
thibired againſt a Governor, Conſul, or Perſon of any degree of Rank, in foreign ſer- 
ce, to tranſmit him a Copy of ſuch Complaint to anſwer, before he is precipitately re- 
pled. How then Mr, Kirke came to be ſo unfortunate a victim, he is at a loſs to ac- 
nt for.

SIR, Whitehall, 2d January, 1767 

55S HAVE received your letter of the 11th ORober®, praying that 

his Majeſty would be graciouſly pleaſed to order one of his ſhips to con 
duct you home, and have laid the ſame before the King. 

I am to acquaint you, that his Majeſty has not thought it proper t 
grant your requeſt ; and muſt obſerve to you, that it is not uſual to gran 
to Conſuls, even the moſt approved, the favour of a King's ſhip, to con 
duct them to, or from the place of their reſidence, except it happen 
that ſome of his Majeſty's ſhips are going, or returning.—lt is therefor 
his Majeſty's pleaſure that you ſhould return home without delayt, b 
ſuch conveyance as you can procure, 

I am, SIR, 
Your moſt obedient humble ſervant, 


Robert Kirke, Eſq; Algiers,  SHELBURNE 


Should have been 31ſt October. See Conſul Kirke's Leer, Appendix, page 4 
to which the above is an Anſwer. 

+ Mr. Kirke immediately obeyed the mandate, but has hitherto remained withot 
eicher heating or recompence.

Conſul Kirke's Letter to the Right Hon. the Earl of Shelburne. 


MY. Lonn .,. + Algiers, 5th April, 1767. 
IN the laſt letter I had the honour of writing to your Lordſhip, 
dated 26th December, 1766, I gave your Lordſhip an account of the 


breach of the peace between this Regency and the Venetians, when 


Commodore Bubeck was here, who failed, as I therein mentioned for 
Mahon, where he has remained ever ſince, diſpatching expreſſes to his 
own Court, and other parts, but particularly to this place, addreſſed to 
their late Conſul Capriata, who has been indefatigable in their ſervice, 
and has juſt obtained a ceſſation of hoſtilities for four months, in which 
time they hope to renew their peace. 

Soon after the commencement of hoſtilities, ſeveral Cruiſers ſailed 
from this Port in queſt of their ſhipping ; the major part of whom were 
forced back by bad weather, and other accidents, before their Cruiſes 
were out; and we have only an account of their having taken one veſſel 


loaded with raiſins, and that the Algerine Rais afterwards got into Tu- 
nis, but no certain advice of the prize being with him, 

Two other Cruiſers failed juſt before the ceſſation was agreed to; 
what damage they will be able to do, is as yet uncertain; and three 
others are juſt gone out, with poſitive directions not to commit any 
hoſtilities againſt the Venetians till further orders. 

The veſſel by whom I convey this letter was inſtantly Aifpatched with 


an expreſs from Mr. Capriata to Commodore Bubeck at Mahon, ac- 


quainting him of the Dey's having agreed to the four months ceſſation ; 
and I am told, though I cannot in this ſhort time be quite poſitive, that 
ſome of the terms are as follows, viz. 
| The Venetians are to ſend a veſſel with warlike ſtores, as demanded, 
upon this Dey's fu ſt acceſſion, 
To pay 50, ooo Sequins in like manner as they did to the late Dey, 
Baba Ally, when their peace was firſt ſettled, and to pay 12,000 Sequins 
annually inſtead of 10,000. 

The Algerines to make prize of the Venetian cargo, if carried into 


Tunis ; but to give up the mariners, and reſtore the veſſel,

MV LORD, Ciaſtle Yard, Holborn, zoth December, 1767 
II is one of the greateſt ſatisfactions I am ar preſent capable 
receiving, after having been traduced and vilified by Commodore Hz 
riſon, in the moſt extravagant and injurious manner, by a Miſrepre 
ſentation of my Conduct at Algiers (in his Letter of the 23d April, 176 
to the Secretary of the Admiralty, as alſo in that of the 18th June f 
| Jowing to the Right Hon. Mr. Secretary Conway“) and my Employme 
itſelf [the ſtaff of life] taken from me in conſequence of it, to find n 
ſelf capable of invalidating the Complaints the Commodore has thaug 
proper to exhibit againſt me; being fully perſuaded, from the conſcio 
neſs of my own innocence, and the ſpirit of thoſe letters, which ſo 
verely reflect upon it, that when this whole Affair ſhall have been th 
roughly examined, your Lordſhip will diſcover nothing but ſcurrili 
inconſiſtences, and abſurdities at the bottom. . 

I am extremely ſorry to find myſelf neceſſarily obliged to treſpals 
your Lordſhip's time, by animadverting on any particular parts of t 
general abuſe level'd at my private Character (an abuſe which ſo mu 
diſcredits the Author) before I proceed to invalidate what more eſſe 
tially regards the honour of my Sovereign, and the public welfare; 
the tenor of Commodore Harriſon's Charge makes it unavoidable, 
declares, in the very out-ſet of his complaint, againſt what he is plea 
to call contempluous Treatment and Impertinence, that “ he can mi 
« the truth of it appear from the teſtimony of Capt. Gower, of

Lordſhip to ſmile at ſo groſs an abſurdity, without taking needleſs pains

done me juſtice againſt my ſuperiors (of what rank ſoever) I had but done 
juſtice to thoſe ſalutary laws to acknowledge it. The truth, however, 
my Lord, is, that though the Commodor: has made me boaſt of three 
or four victories, over perſons of high rank in the Navy, I never have, 
as yet, tried any Cauſe at my own Suit, conſequently have had no vic- 
tory of this ſort to boaſt of, over any perſon whatever; though here I 
cannot avoid remarking, that if, over a friendly glaſs I had ever open'd 
my mind unguardedly on this, or any other ſubje&, and could poſlibly 
have made the boaſt I am charged with (as agreeable to the old proverb 


„ in vino veritas“ I might be ſuppoſed to do) yet, the retailing, exag- 


gerating, and perverting every thing drop'd at a table in confidence; a

ber, 1765, to the 24th following) received daily the higheſt civilities, 
as it is a flagrant violation of the laws of hoſpitality, ſo, with the diſ- 
n it can never give credit to any man's Mord of Honour. |

ference to thoſe of my native country, falls, of its own accord, to the

effect of private pique, or ſiniſter views; the natural intereſt and amity 


of ſtates being (as I humbly preſume) evidently ſupported, rather, by 


meaſures of integrity and juſtice, than by thoſe of artifice and cunning, 

My interrupting the Commodore in his remonſtrances and telling him

wiſe much miſrepreſented, I only | took notice to the Commodore, as | 
apprehended my duty required (not imagining when I was ſent to Al- 
giers as an agent, I could be intended for a cypher only) that the Con- 
ſul was the proper channel for negotiations to paſs through, more eſpe- 


__ cially thoſe relative to the fundamental laws and cuſtoms, not only of 


my own country, but likewiſe of the place I reſided 3 in, which being 
my immediate duty to ſtudy, I might naturally be ſuppoſed to be bel 
acquainted with; and the conſequence the Commodore too haſtily de- 
duces, of my preferring the intereſt of Algiers to that of my native 
country, is not only very unjuſt, but very unnatural,

me) to find my beſt endeavours to eultivate, on the firm footing of ſtri&

of my native Country ; a moſt extraordinary ſuppoſition indeed ! but an 
attention to the Circumſtances of this Affair, together with an inſpec-

of Cancellarea, at Algiers, will fully demonſtrate, that in every Civil 
Matter, I have always maturely deliberated, and acted as the unbiaſſed 
force of Reaſon has fairly determined me.

MY LORD, Caſtle Yard, Holborn, zoth December, 1767. 
HAVING received, on the 13th of laſt month, from your 
Lordſhip's office, an extract of Lieut. Governor Johnſton's letter, dated 
the 29th June, 1767, to your Lordſhip, which mentions a diſpatch by 
him received a few days before from Mr. Sampſon at Algiers, whereby 


he ſays, *©* he finds from Commodore Spry, and ſome officers with him, 
„that by intrigues and preſents I had contrived to prepoſſeſs the Dey of 
e Algiers and his Miniſters ſo much in my favour, that Mr. Spry met 


with iafinite difficulty in prevailing upon the Dey to allow Mr. Samp- 


« ſon to act till the arrival of Mr, Frazer.” 
I humbly beg leave to auſwer, that the charge of my contriving by 


nei“ intrigues and preſents to prepoſſeſs the Dey and his Miniſters in my 
1 © favour,” is only the aſſertion of a fact exiſting in imagination, and 


that the inference drawn from it, of Commodore Spry's thereby meet- 
“ing with infinite difficulty in prevailing on the Dey to allow Mr. 
« Sampſon to act till the arrival of Mr. Frazer,” is, altogether, miſ-

repreſented. The fact being, That Commodore Spry introduced Mr. 
Sampſon to the Dey, not as a perſon to act during my abſence, but as 
full Conſul, on which the uſual ſalute was demanded, and given accor- 
dingly. Soon after which, the cuſtomary preſents were demanded, 
This occaſioned the difficulties ſuggeſted to have ariſen ; whereupon, the 
Commodore then aſſured the Dey, that if Mr. Frazer, who was fixed on 
by his Britannic Majeſly to reſide as his Conſul in Algiers, did not ar- 
rive in three months, he (Mr. Sampſon) ſhould make the preſents uſual 
on the arrival of a new Conſul. 

Lieut, Governor Johnſton proceeds in the next place to obſerve to your 
Lordſhip, he believes it paſt a doubt, that from private views and piques, 
I have greatly prevented the Ifland of Minorca from receiving the ſup- 

plies ſo neceſſary for the ſubſillence both of his Majeſty's troops and ſub- 
jects in it. In anſwer to which, I flatter myſelf, I may, without vanity, 
aſſert, that I always exerted my moſt ſtrenuous endeavours to procure 
Grain, not only for the troops, but alſo for the inhabitants of Minorca; 
and that in conſequence thereof, many loadings were obtained for their 
reſpective uſes, as will manifeſtly appear, not only by the copy of the 
Clearance Book kept by me in the Cancellarea of my Office (in pur- 
ſuance of a letter I received from the Right Hon. the Earl of Halifax, 
of the 18th January, 1765, fignifying his Majeſty's commands to that 
effect) and the letters that paſſed on that ſubject between myſelf and the 
Lieut, Governor, but alſo by the letters from the Magiſtrates of Mahon, 
and their ſeveral grateful acknowledgments under their public ſeal to me 
thereon. And if my information be right, more Grain was freely ob- 
tained in my Conſulſbip, not only for the uſe of the Iſland of Minorca 
in general, but Gibraltar alſo, than was permitted to be exported from 
Algiers within the ſpace of /x Years preceding. And that all Grain per- 
mitted to be exported by the Conſuls or Merchants of any other nation 
whatever, nay even by the Turks themſelves, was obtained merely by 
dint of money and large preſents to the leading people. |

thought proper to animadvert on a proteſt which was preſented him by 
one of the owners of Juan Netto's veſlel, and which the Lieut. Governor 
has alſo thought proper to mention to your Lordſhip, as only one Pruf

That on the 13th October, 1766, Juan Netto, ſen. Maſter of the 
Brig St. Juan Baptiſta, anchored in the Mole of Algiers, but never came 
to the Conſulary Houſe as he ought. On the 14th, in the morning, 
hearing nothing from him, I ſent my Drugoman to find him out, with 
whom he returned about 10 or 11 o'clock; upon my aſking Netto, If he 
had any letters? he anſwered ©** No, excezt for the Conſul; to whom 
I replied © 7 am the Conſul, and that I expected he would deliver the 
«© letters to me. To this he anſwered, with a ſneer, ** No, you are 
„ not the Conſul, Mr. Sampſon is Conſul;”” to which I replied, * There 
* was no other Conſul but myſelf; and that I infiſted on the letters 
e being delivered to me as »/ual.” I then aſked him for his Medi- 
terrancan Paſs, and Bill of Health; upon which he ſaid, be had left 
them on Board; I then told him, he ſhould not go out of my houſe till 
I was afſared of having his letters; not only to cauſe them to be delivered 
as cuſtomary, but that I might ſee if any were ox is MajesTY's Service 
(looking upon it to be my indiſpenſable duty to open them if they were). 


But he appearing determined, neither to produce his letters or papers, I 


told him he ſhould not go out of my houſe till he conſented to my having 
the letters; and then I ordered the door to be ſhut. On which he went 
into the ſquare, or area, where he continued ſmoaking his pipe near an 
hour; and apprehending, as I ſuppoſe, I would not recede from my reſo- 
lution, he, on my repeatedly urging him, to let me have his Mediter- 
ranean Paſs, Bill of Health, and alſo deliver me his letters, and act as 


euſtomary, ſaid, if I would conſent to it, he would go and fetch the 


ſame, I told him, his behaviour was ſuch, that I neither could truſt to 
him nor his word; and therefore would take my Drugoman and Clerk, 
and go « on board with him ; ; to which he conſenting, we went on board

And thus, my Lord, having given your Lordſhip a true and particu- 
lar account of the facts and circumſtances relative to this unexpected Pro- 
teſt of Juan Netto, as they really fell out, I beg your Lordſhip's leave, 


to make. ſome few remarks and reflexions thereon. And firſt, that my 


detaining Juan Netto in my houſe at Algiers for about an hour was only 


to induce him to enter his veſſel at the Conſulary Office as uſual; and _ 


for my obtaining a fight of the letters he told me he had brought with 
him for the Conſul, left any of them ſhould have been on his Majeſty's 
ſervice, as I had great reaſon to be in daily expectation of hearing from 
your Lordſhip's Office, and, at the ſame time apprehending it my parti- 
cular and indiſpenſable duty, to carry on his Majeſty's ſervice with 
punctuality and diſpatch till the arrival of a ſucceſſor. 

Juan Netto's charges of my having taken out of his hands, both the 
Dey's and Mr. Sampſon's letters, and preceeding after reading them to 
his Highneſs to make the remar{:s he pretends in his Proteſt to be made 
by me thereon, in order, as he ſuggeſts, to prevent his getting a cargo 


of Corn for his Majeſty's Troops at Minorca, or my obſtructing him in 


any wiſe therein, from any prejudice conceived againſt him on account 


of Mr. Alexiano his paſſenger, or from any other motive whatever (or 
my acting in this affair otherwiſe than as my narrative explains) is utterly 


falſe; as will more clearly appear, when I acquaint your Lordſhip, 
that the Lieut. Governor's letter now in my poſſeſſion is wrote in Spa- 
niſh, a language I am an entire ſtranger to, and therefore carries its 
own confutation with it. 

And I cannot avoid obſerving to your Lordſhip, that Juan Netto's 


making his Proteſt againſt me (which I humbly apprehend, on due exa- 


mination, will appear he had not any right to do) on the very day that 
{ embarked on my return to England, and not before (although he had 
ſuch a length of time, as upwards of 60 days, to do it in, as before ſet 


forth) and thereby depriving me of an opportunity, not only of furniſhing 


myſelf with vouchers; but alſo of producing witneſſes on the ſpot ne-

And having now, my Lord, gone through my remarks on the unwar. 
rantable behaviour of Juan Netto, with reſpect to his Proteſt againſt me, 
I humbly beg leave to take notice of the circumſtance of the Lieutenant 
Governor's Seal of Office (a mark of diſtinction never made uſe of be. 
fore) being affixed to the letter wrote by him to the Dey, and incloſed 
in that directed to Mr. Sampſon in favour of Dr. John Olivar ; and the 
omiſſion of that mark of diſtinction on the letter ſent by Tudury, and 
which accompanied that of the Magiſtrates of Mahon on fo preſſing an 
exigence, as that of the public ſervice ſet forth in that letter. 

And as Lieut. Governor Johnſton has thought fit to mention the Pro. 
teſt of Juan Netto (which 1 hope I have fully anſwered) as only one 
proof of many which might be given of what he (the Lieut, Governor) 
has advanced in his letter, I humbly beg leave to obſerve, that this re- 
mark of the Lieut. Governor carries, I preſume, ſo manifeſt a deſign to 
Prejudice your Lordſhip againſt me, that I flatter myſelf it will ſerve ef- 
fectually to defeat fo ill- natured a purpoſe; my conſcience, at the ſame 
time aſſuring me, that I ſhall be able to make an ample defence againſt 
any accuſation the Lieut. Governor can poſſibly exhibit againſt me. 

I have the honour to be, 
My Lord, 
Your Lordſhip's moſt a and 
devoted humble ſervant, 
ROBERT KIRKE.

Algiers, 12th January, 1767. 3 Vives, Maſter of the Snow, Pro- 
vidence, relative to Mr. Theodore Alexiano, being admitted 
to Pratic at Mahon, without the uſual and —— Bill of 
Health. 

JAMES VIVES, Maſter of the Snow, Providence, of Port Mahon, 
declares, That he was in the Pratic Houſe at Mahon, when Mr, Theo- 
dore Alexiano came in, and that he declared to the Magiſtrates of 
Health, that he came from Algiers without a Bill of Health ; that there 


was a great number of people in the houſe, and that it was publickly 


faid, Mr. Alexiano had no Bill of Health, becauſe he had quarrePd 
with the Conſul, and was therefore obliged to run away from Algiers. 
That Mr. Theodore Alexiano likewiſe aſſured the people at Mahon, 
that nobody would be permitted to load at Algiers, before Commodore 
Spry and the new Conſul arrived there. Given under my hand, in the 
City of Algiers, the 12th day of January, 1767. 

(Signed) JAMES VIVES.

BY the Captain of the Port I have the opportunity to inform 
you, that the Ship (under my command) is a Store-Ship, belonging to 


his Britannic Majeſty, loaded with Stores for his Majeſty's Ships at An- 


tigua, and Jamaica, and was ſtopped by an Algerine Ship, commanded 
by Ahmet Benzerzo, Rais, on ſuſpicion of my Commiſſion, which he

5 SIR, 55 Algiers, 12th February, 1766. 

4 48 ths French Veſſel by whom I ſend this to Marſeilles will be 
under way in an hour, I have only time to acquaint you, that on Mon- 
day laſt, Lieut, Robert Deans, commanding his Majeſty's Store-Ship, 
the Florida, was brought in here, by ſome Turks that had been put on 
board her, out of one of their Cruiſers, who fell in with the Florida of 

Cape St. Vincent, I immediately waited on the Dey with Lieut; Deans, 

and repreſented this affair to him with all its circumſtances, who is greatly 

chagrined at a thing of the kind happening, made every conceſſion in 

His power, has promiſed to puniſh ſeverely the Rais, or Captain of the 

Cruiſer upon his arrival from his cruize, which, I ſuppoſe, will be the 

loſs of his head: the Dey is alſo ready to make any other ſatis faction the 

King of Great Britain may require. Lieut, Deans having, in his paſlage 

here, prepared the major part of his narrative, will, I believe, be able 


to ſend it away to the Lords of the Admiralty by this opportunity, to 


which I mult beg leave to refer, and ſhall do myſelf the honour to writ

SIR, | Algiers, 13th February, 1766. 


SINCE my laſt, of yeſterday's date, by the French veſſel that 
ſailed the inſtant I had ſealed my letter, relative to the Florida Store- 
Ship being brought in here, the Dey ſent to my houſe the Packet you'll 


receive herewith, and deſired it might be forwarded to the King of Eng- 


land as ſoon as poſſible; but as it was ſealed (after their manner) and as 
the Dey did not communicate the Contents to me, I thought I ſhould 


have done a very wrong and unjuſtifiable act to have opened it, to be ex- 
plained for my information, and afterwards tranſmitted it with the ori- 


ginal to you, Sir; I muſt therefore requeſt the favour of having the con- 
tents communicated to me, in order to form a more certain judgment of 
their manner of acting + ; this I ſhould not have mentioned, I had not al- 
ways received through the hands of the Secretary of State, a copy of 
ſuch letters as the King is pleaſed to honour the Dey with. 

In themſelves, they appear truly diſconcerted with the proceedings of 
their Rais, or Captain; and as I ſaid yeſterday, I believe he will be 
but roughly handled upon his return into Port; the reſult of all which

Conſul Kirke's Letter to Governor Irwin. 

SIR, : Algiers, 16th February, 1766. 
LIEUT, Robert Deans, commanding his *ajeſty's Store-Ship, 
Florida, being unfortunately brought in here by the fooliſhneſs and ob- 
ſtinacy of one of their cruiſers, belonging to this place, and though the 
Dey inſtantly ordered the Ship to be diſcharged, gave the Commander 
all the aſſiſtance in his power, behaved with all imaginable civility, and 
aſſured me he would properly puniſh the Rais for his bad conduct, yet 
it has given the Commander of the Florida ſome trouble, and may pro- 
bably occafion his ſtopping at Gibraltar, if ſo, give me leave to recom- 
mend him to your favor and aſſiſtance, particularly, in regard to Pratic, 
as we are in perfect health here at preſent. For any thing elſe, as a 
King's officer, I am perſuaded I need ſay no more to General Irwin, 
mly embrace the opportunity to congratulate him on the ſafe arrival of 
himſelf and family, and to aſſure him how much I am, with great 
reſpect, his | = | 
Moſt obedient and moſt humble ſervant, 

His Excellency Major General ROBERT KIRKE. 
John Irwin, Gibraltar, . 8

Marine. I muſt own, I found ſome few obſtacles in bringing this about, 
chiefly occaſioned, as J have ſince been informed, by Benzerzo's having 
powerful friends about the Dey, his being chief owner of the cruiſer 
himſelf; and what is ſtill ſtronger, his ſtanding indebted at the King's 
houſe upwards of 1000. ſterling, and no other means whatever, except 
by the chance of cruiſing, to diſcharge that demand. 

The Dey has likewiſe offered any other ſatisfaction the King of En- 
gland may require; but as Benzerzo has met many unavoidable and un- 
foreſeen misfortunes, and is at preſent in diſtreſſed circumſtances, added 
to his having a large family, wholly dependant on his labours alone, 
has induced me to let the matter reſt here, till I am honoured with your 
commands upon the head, And ſhould I have erred in this, or in any 
other of my proceedings, ſince I have had the honour of reſiding here as 
Conſul, I hope it will be looked over favourably, the nature and diſpo- 
ſition of theſe people conſidered. 

I am ſorry J cannot be ſo punctual in writing as J could wiſh, as there 
is no direct conveyance from hence, and frequent embarpo's laid on by 
this State, which often continue 40 days. 

It is with pleaſure I take leave to acquaint you, that I ſtand full as 
well with the preſent Dey, and his Miniſtry, as with the former, I 
have the honour to ſubſcribe myſelf, with great reſpe&, | 

SIR, Your molt obedient and moſt humble ſervant, 
ROBERT KIRKE.

Letter from Lieut. Robert Deans to Conſul Kirke. | 
DEAR SIR, Gibraltar, 18th April, 1766. 
I HAVE now got my orders to proceed on my voyage; and as 


the encloſed, I apprehend, is confined to the Mediterranean, which I 
ſhall leave the firſt Levant Wind, I return it with thanks. I ſent by the 
Centurion your ſhirt and cravat, which Ship, when ſhe ſailed from here, 


was to go to Algiers, but I find was forced to make the beſt of her way 

to Minorca, vet, by my letters from England, I think Mr. Harriſon 

will pay you a viſit, I am, with my beſt compliments to Mrs. Kirke and 
Miſs Coxeter, Dear SIR, 

Your very humble Servant, 

To Robert Kirke, Eſq; 

His Britannic Majeſty's Conſul at Algiers. | 

Tran- 


ROBERT DEANS.

IE OO ET IN dt te 


r 
Tranſlation of the Dey of Algiers's Orders to his Cruiſers, to 


render every aſſiſtance in their power to Lieut. Robert Deans, 
Commander of his Majeſty's Store Ship, Florida, viz, 


WITH the help of the Almighty and Powerful God, 


To all you that are Raiſes of Cruiſers belonging to 1 Peace, 
and Health, and the mercy of God be upon you, now and ever, 
Amen. 

The occaſion of this, is, to let you hom that the Bearer of this is 
an Engliſh Captain, that was ſent here by Rais Benzerzo, thinking him 
a prize, and after his arrival here, we have reſtored him, and ſet him at 

| liberty ; and therefore all our cruiſers, that ſhall meet 
him in his way to Gibraltar, are ſtrictly charged not 
to meddle with him, nor hinder, or moleſt him, but 
to aſſiſt him with all that is in their power. Done by

| SIR, Algiers, 2oth April; 1767; 
* HAVING bell a certain account of your ſafe arrival at 


Mahon with Mr. Sampſon, who, if my information be right, was to act 
here whilſt I returned to England, to anſwer ſome complaints made 
* againſt me by Commodore Harriſon, when Commander in Chief in 
© if theſe Seas, but finding by the French gazettes, that Mr. Fraſer, late 
Conſul at Tripoly, is appointed full Conſul in my ſtead, it gives me 
room to apprehend, I ſhall not have the pleaſure of ſeeing you ſo ſoon 
as I expected ; I therefore take leave to congratulate you on this impor- 
„ tant Command, and your ſafe arrival at Mahon; and from the know- : 
ö. ledge I had of you at Louiſbourg upwards of 20 years ſince, when with 
h MW the gallant Sir Peter Warren, I am perſuaded every good will hap- 
pen. to his Britannic Majeſty's ſubjects under your protection; and as 
there are many things at Algiers worthy your particular Attention, per- | 
-mit me to aſſure you, that your preſence here would afford me the ; 
- | greateſt: happineſs, not only as an individual, but for the general good 
1 | of his Majeſty's Service, and the promotion of Trade and Commerce in 
„mis place, that has, I fear, been much neglected for years paſt : but if 
„vou ſhould not chuſe to enter on any buſineſs, as I am told your health 
e | has been much impaired fince in theſe ſeas, change of air may be uſe- 
ful, and you may be aſſured, Sir, that myſelf and family will do all in 
our power to render Algiers as agreeable as any part of Barbary can 
be to an Engliſh conſtitution. I am, with a tender of my beſt ſervices

SIR, | | Algiers, 22d May, 1767. 

IN anſwer to your Excellency's letter of yeſterday's date, which 

was delivered me this morning by Captain Dickſon's Servant, I beg 

leave to aſſure your Excellency, that I did particularly mention the fol- 

lowing things to your Excellency on the 2oth and 21ſt of April laſt, 

when I had the honour of waiting on your Excellency on board the Jer- 
ſey, viz, 

That the accounts depending between Mr. Leeds Booth, Pratic Ma- 
ſter at Gibraltar, and Aly Hogia, a Turk of high rank here, and alſo 
thoſe of Mr. Bentaleb, a Mooriſh Merchant of this place, might be 
fairly acjuſted and ſettled, and the ballance paid, particularly that 
which appeared due to Aly Hogia; that the paſs granted at Mahon to 
Michael Chefallano for the Pollacca, Sta. Catharina, might be taken 
from him, or ſuch other puniſhment inflicted, as the nature of the caſe 
might deſerve, on account of his behaviour when here in September 


laſt; that Mr. Alexiano, the Pratic Maſter of Mahon, who returned 


here again in the Jerſey, might be dealt with as the nature of his beha- 


viour and offence might alſo require. That the affair relative to the 


Spaniſh Zebeque, loaded with oil, &c. taken by the Tuniſtans, in 
company with one of theſe Cruiſers, and afterwards claimed in a letter 
I received from Mr. Edward Clarke, Secretary to his Excellency Gover- 


nor Johnſton, dated the 24th July laſt, might be looked into, and ſet- 


tled; the particulars of all which are regularly entered in the Regiſter 
books, delivered up by me to Mr. Sampſon the 1oth inftant, and which 
I had the honour to read over to your Excellency on board the Jerſey. 
It likewiſe appears to me neceſſary to mention the following things to 
your Excellency, viz. 

That Mr. Sampſon may be directed never to iſſue any Paſſports and 
certificates to Rais Mahomet Benzerzo, late Commander of the Cruiſer 


that brought ! into this Port his Majeſty's Store Ship the Florida, com- 
 manged

manded by Lieut. Deans, having myſelf been ſeveral times applied to

and had I ſtayed here to the day of my death, I never would have iſſued, 

unleſs I had received his Majeſty” s commands for that purpoſe. Your 
Excellency may likewiſe ſec it neceſſary, & inquire into the particulars 
of the Portugueſe taken out of ay Engliſh veſſel belonging to Pool, 

called the ſhip Joſeph, Joſeph Crew, Maſter, which were ſometime 
fince brought in here by Ibrahim Rais, Commander of a Zebeque 
Cruiſer, of this place, which veſſel and Rais returned from Tunis on 
the 19th April laſt, the day the Jerſey anchored in this Bay, 

I muit alſo obſerve, that few, I may, I believe, venture to affirm, 
not above two of the ſeveral Merchant veſſels now in Port under Engliſh 
colours, appear to have a right thereto, or to their paſſes, being foreign 
. bottoms, not made free, many of the Commanders Foreigners, and few 
of them navigated according to the act of Parliament and orders relative 
thereto. 

The manner likewiſe in which the Governor and Magiſtrates of Mahon 
apply to this State vox Corn, appears to me alſo irregular; and 
though I never could obtain any /arisfa&ory Anfaver to my ſeveral letters 
to them on that Head, yet I have exerted my beſt offices in their behalf, 
and if my information be right, have obtained more Grain for the uſe, 
not only of the inhabitants of ht and, but Gibraltar alſo, than has been 
extratied from this country for TwenTY Years paſt; and if the proper 
methods could be hit on to put theſe buſineſſes on a regular and certain 
Feoting, much benefit might ariſe thereby, not only to England, but 
the Garriſon of Gibraltar, and the Iſland of Minorca in particular. 


All my correſpondence on this point is likewiſe entered in the Regiſter 
books delivered up. to Mr. Sampſon. | 
Having been withour a Clerk, or any other aſſiſtance, PE Mr, 
Sampſon took on him the charge of this Conſulate, I flatter myſelf your 
Excellency will excuſe this letter being copied by my Siſter, 
I have the honour to be, with great reſpect, | 
SIR, | | 
Your Excellency's moſt obedient, and 
Moſt humble Servant, 
ROBERT KIRKE,

PS. The anſwer to your Excellency's other letter 
relative to our going home, I hope to be able

His Excellency Commodore Spry, 


SIR, | Algiers, 23d May, 1967. 
YOUR Excellency's Letter of the 21ſt, relative to my going 
home, was delivered me yeſterday forenoon by Captain Dickſon's ſer- 
vant, accompanying another of the ſame date, relative to the public af- 
fairs at Algiers, which I immediately anſwered, This therefore ſerves 
in ſome meaſure to lay before your Excellency my preſent diſtreſsful 
State, and the Obſtacles that preſent themſelves to the ſeveral offers your 
Excellency is pleaſed to make me, viz. 

As to our going to Mahon or Marſeilles in the Jerſey, that could, I 
apprehend, facilitate the getting home only of my/elf, and as you can 
be no ſtranger to the State of Health and Situation of my wife, who is in 
the eighth month of her pregnancy, added to my having two young 
children, a ſiſter, and a large family in this Barbary Country, whom I 
cannot think of leaving behind me, as Mr, Fraſer is appointed FULL Con- 
SUL &, and therefore no likelihood of my returning to Algiers again; and 
were we carried to Mahon, or Marſeilles, the remedy would prove worſe 
than the diſeaſe, being already ſo much hurt in my circumſtances, that 
it puts it out of my power to attempt any new, or additional expence, 
which muſt neceſſarily enſue on our going to either of thoſe places. 

The ſame objection ſubſiſts in regard to the Hon. Capt. Cornwallis 
carrying us either to Gibraltar, Cadiz, or Liſbon, even had I not re- 
ceived a letter from him, in anſwer to one I did myſelf the pleaſure of 
writing him on the 24th April laſt, Copies of which are incloſed*. 

You alſo know, Sir, that upon your arrival, I had two as good houſes, 


the one in town, and the other in the country, as molt in theſe parts, 


. 


& Notwithfianding this was a fat notoriouſly known, and which his Excellency Mr, 
Spry had read in the Engliſh Gazette at Mahon before his coming to Algiers; yet his Ex- 
cellency judged it neceflary, as before ſet forth, to introduce Mr. Sampſon as full Conſul, 
the more effeQually 0 entangle the deſtructive Miſchief that had been ſet on foot.

your Excellency's arrival with Mr. Sampſon was only the 19th of laſt 
month, when his Grace the Duke of Richmond's Letter, incloſfing the

hands by Mr. Sampſon, ever ſince which, no man living, circumſtanced 
as I was, and am, could poſſibly be mere diligent than I have been to 
obey my King's Commands in giving up the Office, &c. &c. nay, my 
all is at ſtake in regard to getting ſpecdily home, having neither em- 


ployment here, or money, except by the ſale of my effects, to maintain 


my innocent and numerous family, ſet aſide the light I muſt appear in to 
the Public, till the waoLe TxuTH comes out. And you will alſo pleaſe

5 Algiers, 24th April, 1767. 
CONSUL KIRKE preſents his compliments to the Hon, Cap- 


tain Cornwallis, is extremely ſorry he had not the honour of his company 


to dinner yeſterday, but hopes for that favour this day, to-morrow, or 


any other day, and as often as he chuſes during his ſtay in this country; 


and that he would be pleaſed to bring with him ſuch gentlemen belong- 
ing to the ſhip as may be agreeable to him. 
The Hon. William Cornwallis, Captain 

of his Majeſty's Ship N 

Algiers Bay. 


CAPTAIN CORNWALLIS preſents his compliments to Mr. Kirke, 
is extremely obliged to him for his very polite note, but (having no 
public bufineſs to tranſact) and having heard many things reflecting on 
Mr. Kirke's conduct ſince he has been in the Mediterranean, Captain 
Cornwallis begs leave to defer the pleaſure of making an acquaintance 
with Mr. Kirke at preſent, till he has had an opportunity of clearing 
thoſe matters up; after which, Captain Cornwallis will, en all oc- 
cafions, have great pleaſure in paying his reſpects to Mr. e 

Guadalupe, z5th April, 1767.

100 wiſhed the Conſul and his whole Family might be no more heard of. 

Captain Cornwallis acted an honeſt part at leaſt, But this note plainly ſhews the 
reaſon of that rancour that marked the conduct of Commodore Spry ; Mr. Harriſon was a 
Captain of 2 Man of War, and the infection ran through the Corps, Whoever has ſerved 
any time in the Navy, will have no difficulty to comprehend this part of the buſineſs 
fully. But as Mr. Kirke ever held the Cornwallis's in the higheſt Eſtimation, in point of 
Homour and Tzu TH, he cannot help flattering himſelf (having never as yet been allowed 
any opportunity to ſet this unfortunate and miſtaken affair in a clear light by any other 
means than this Sketch of his Caſe) he ſhall ſhortly have the pleaſure of ſeeing Captain 
Corpwallis (if alive) at No. 31, Cattle Yard, Holborn, in acknowledgment of the po- 
ce lite note, as he was pleaſed to term it, which he received when in Alziers; being

WHEN a worthy and upright Judge, who had always ſhewn the moſt 


ſacred regard to the Laws of his Country, had been groſsly and pub- 


lickly reflected on, it became a National Affair; and therefore the fol- 


lowing remarkable Caſe of the Court Martial held on Board His Ma- 


jeſty's Ship, Prince of Orange, at Deptford, on Friday the 16th May, 
1746, for the trial of Mr. Leſtock, and & others, was given to the public, 
and was as follows, viz. 


Lieut. Frye, of his Majeſty Navy, in 8 1745-6, recovered 
1000. damages againſt Sir Chaloner Ogle, in a ſuit at law, for a 
Sentence prfſed againſt bim by a Court Martial held in the Weſt Indies, 
whereof” Sir Chaloner 'was Prefident ; and it being the opinion of the 
Court on the ſaid trial at law, That Mr. Frye had a right to bring 
his Aion againſt any Member that compoſed ſaid Court Martial, he 
therefore in conſequence thereof, very juſtly cauſed a Writ to iſue from 
the Court of Common Pleas againſt Rear Admiral Mayne, who was 
then Preſident of the Court Martial fitting for the Trial of Mr. Leftock, 
Sc. Mr. Mayne hawing been a Member of the former Court Martial 
held on Mr. Frye in the Weſt Indies. Admiral Mayne was thereupon 
arreſted, or ſerved with proceſs at the ſuit of Mr. Frye. This circum- 
france brought on, the following very e ue; of ſaid

The Court being aſſembled as uſual, 

THE Preſident acquainted the Court that he had been yeſterday after- 
noon, after the Court was up, arreſted by a writ at the ſuit of Mr. Frye, 
for a ſentence paſſed on him at a Court Martial held on him in the Welt 
Indies.— Capt. Hamilton, one of the Members of the Court, moved 
that the writ might be read: the Judge Advocate accordingly read the 
ſame, being a writ of Capias, iſſued out of the Court of Common Pleas, 
atteſted May 12 and returnable June 1, againſt Sir Chaloner Ogle, 
Perry Mayne, &c. perſons who were Members of that Court Martial. 
Then the Court, on the motion of Capt. Hamilton, came to the fol- 


lowing reſolutions, viz. 


Re/olved, That it appears the higheſt indighity offered to the Court, 
and through this Court to every other Court of Judicature, that 1s or 
may hereafter be formed in this Kingdom; and the higheſt infraction of 
the prerogative of the Lord High Admiral, and of the ſtatute law of this 
Realm, to arreſt or ſerve any writ of Capias upon the Preſident, or any 
Member of this Court now ſitting, or of any other Court Martial ; and 
therefore the Court unanimouſly reſolve to deſiſt farther — on 
this trial till ſatisfaction be made for this high inſult. 

Re/elved, That this Court make repreſentation by letter to the Lord 
High Admiral, of the high infringement made on his prerogative, by 
arreſting the Preſident of this Court, duly aſſembled, by virtue of his 
authority; and that the Court do adjourn till Thurſday morning 9 
o'clock, to give time for every Member to deliberate upon proper me- 
thods for obtaining ſatisfaction for the high inſult on their Preſident, 
from all perſons, how high ſoever in rank or office, who have fet on foot 


this arreſt, or in any degree promoted or adviſed i it. 


Theſe reſolutions being ſigned by the Preſident, and the ſeveral Mem- 
bers of the Court, the Preſident directed it to be encloſed in a letter, 
and delivered by the Judge Advocate to the Lords of the Admiralty, 


Then the Cqurt adjourned till Monday morning 9 o'clock, and from 
thence to Thurſday the 22d,

On the 224, a Letter from Mr. Corbett, Secretary to the Lords 
Commiſſioners of the Admiralty, encloſing one from the Duke 
of Newcaſtle to their Lordſhips, was read before the Court, 
and are as follows : 
Mr. Corbett's Letter to the Prefident. 


SIR, 5 
HAVING laid the letter of the 16th inſtant, and alſo the reſolu- 
tions of the Court Martial of the ſame date, brought hither by the Judge 
Advocate, before the Lords Commiſſioners of the Admiralty, their Lord- 
ſnips thought the ſaid reſolutions to be a matter of ſuch importance that 
they laid the ſame before his Majeſty. In conſequence of which, their 
Lordſhips received a letter from his Grace the Duke of Newcaſtle, principal 


Secretary of State; and I am commanded to ſend you encloſed an atteſted 
copy thereof, in order to your laying the ſame before the Court Martial at 


your meeting to-morrow. Their Lordſhips make no doubt but that 
every gentleman who is Member of the Court, will be greatly ſatisfied 
with his Majeſty's moſt gracious expreſſions contained in the ſaid letter; 


and as they are aſſured of his Majeſty's protection, and of having full 


ſatisfaction for the late indignity offered them, and that methods will be 


taken to prevent any thing of the like nature for the future, their Lord- 
| ſhips hope they will be unanimous in agreeing to go on immediately in 
hearing and adjuſting the trials that are depending, and to continue fo 


till they are finiſhed. 
I am, your's, &c. 


Admiralty Office, May 21, 1746. „ ©, CORBETT. 


Duke of Newcaſlle*s Letter. 
To my Lords Commiſſioners of the Admiralty. 
I RECEIVED: your Lordſhips letter of the 16th inſtant, with the pa-

prefides at the Court Martial at Deptford, for enquiring into the con- 
duct, &c. of Mr, Leſtock and others, and the reſolutions of the Court 
thereupon, and have laid them before his Majeſty. His Majeſty ex 


preſſed great diſpleaſure at the inſult offered to the Court Martial, by 
which the Military Diſcipline of the Navy is ſo much affected, and the 


King highly diſapproves the behaviour of Lieut. F rye on this occaſion.

(Received May 22, 1745.) HOLLES, NEWCASTLE. 


The Anſwer of the Court Martial to the Letter ſent by Mr. Cor- 
bett, by Order of the Lords of the Admiralty, May 21. 
SIR, | 

WE deſire you would be pleaſed to inform their Lordſhips, that, 
having heard their Lordſhips letter read to us, as well as the authentic 
copy of the letter to their Lordſhips from his Majeſty's principal Secretary 
of State the Duke of Newcaſtle, ſignifying his Majeſty's Royal Aſſurance 
of his moſt gracious Protection, for procuring to us a ſufficient ſatisfafQtion 
for the late indignity offered to us, and that proper methods will be taken 
for preventing any thing of the like nature for the future, according to 


their Lordſhips defire, we are unanimouſly agreed to proceed upon the 


buſineſs of the trials; but think it incumbent upon us to give their 
Lordſhips our reaſons for the ſteps we have already taken, ſubmitting 
them to their Lordſhip's farther conſideration, as this attack appears to 


us of the moſt dangerous conſequence to the ſecurity of the nation, the 


authority of the Lord High Admiral, the privilege of the honourable 


_ Houſe of Commons in Parliament aſſembled, and the prerogative of the 


Crown. | 

Firſt, It appears to us, that Sir John Willes, Knt. Lord Chief Juſticeof the 
Court of Common Pleas, having noregard to the honour and ſafety of his 
Majeſty, the ſecurity of the liberties and properties of his ſubjects, the 
ſupport of the conſtitution, or defence of his dominions, in time of ac- 


| tual war with two powerful nations, favoured, aided and abetted by re- 


bellion at home (contrary to his duty and truſt, and in violation of the 
ſtatute laws of the Realm as well as thoſe eſtabliſhed by uſe and cuſtom 


time immemorial, by which his Majeſty's arms by ſea have been pru- 


dently, 


126 nl 


dently, wiſely and effectually governed, to the great glory of his Majeſty, 
and protection of his ſubjects, as well as in open defiance of his Majeſty's 
commands, in conſequence of an addreſs from the honourable Houſe of 
Commons in Parliament aſſembled) the ſaid Lord Chief Juſtice Willes did 
iſſue his writ, on the 12th day of May, in the 19th year of his preſent 
Majeſty's Reign, to arreſt, ſeize ahd ſecure the perſons of Perry Mayne, 
Eſq; and James Rentone, Eſq; Members conſtituent, and Judges of the 
Court Martial then ſitting, by orders of the Lords Commiſſioners for 
executing the office of Lord High Admiral of Great Britain, and by 
virtue of the power to them given by the Stat. of 13. K. Cha, II. the 
faid Judges being duly qualified as the act requires, and did cauſe the 


ſaid writ to be ſerved on the ſaid Perry Mayne, Eſq; by which craft and 


ſubtle device, as far as in him layeth, he did let, reſpite, and diſturb 


the laws of the land, and, by forcibly taking the Judges, prevent the 


execution of juſtice, and elude the commands of his Majeſty, grounded 


upon the addreſs of the honourable Houſe of Commons in Parliament 
- + aſſembled; and though, in a clauſe ſubjoined to the ſaid writ, it was 


artfully added, that the Preſident is ſerved with this proceſs, to the in- 
tent that he may, by his Attorney, appear in his Majeſty's Court of 
Common Pleas, it appears to us that this evaſion is only provided to 
the intent that we might poſſibly, through overſight, proceed in our ju- 
dicial capacity; and that, by ſome reſerved device, as well our general 


proceedings, as our final determination and ſentence, might be inter- 
preted and declared invalid, void, and none effect, to the entire defeat- 


ing of this ſolemn, grand and national enquiry, 
Secondly, That the grounds for this writ was an action recommended by 


the ſaid Lord Chief Juſtice in open Court, for damages againſt the. ſaid 
Perry Mayne, Eſq; and James Rentone, Eſq; for the ſentence of a Court 


Martial legally held, by virtue of the ſtatute laws of this realm at Ja- 
maica, of which Court the ſaid Perry Mayne, Eſq; and James Rentone, 
Eſq; were Members, Conſtituents or Judges, properly qualified as ſuch 


by the known laws of the realm, as well ſtatutes, as uſe and cuſtom. 


- Thirdly, That in conſequence of the ſaid breach and violation of the 


laws of the Kingdom, as well as inſult to a ſupreme Court of Judicature, 
fitting to determine in the derniere reſort, which by its conſtitution, 
never acknowledged any ſuperior Court, nor any appeal from its ſen- 
-tence, but to his Majeſty's prerogative, as far as in him layeth, the

SOON after hay, the following Papes; ſigned by Admiral Mayne, 
Admiral Byng, and the ſeveral Commanders whoſe Names are ſubſcrib- 
ed thereto, was ſent by them to the Right Hon. Sir John Willes, Knt. 
Lord Chief Juſtice of his . s Court of Common Pleas. 

London Gazette, November 15. 

As akin] is more 3 a Gentleman than to acknowledge him- 
ſelf to be in the wrong as ſoon as he is ſenſible he is ſo, and to be ready 
to make Satisfaction to any perſon he has injured, We therefore whoſe 
Names are under-written, being thoroughly convinced, that we were in- 


tirely miſtaken in the opinion we had conceived of the Lord Chief Juſ- . - 


tice Willes, think ourſelves obliged in Honour as well as Juſtice, to 
make him Satisfaction as far as in our power. And as the Injury we did 
him was of a Public Nature, We do in this Public Manner declare, that 
we are now fatisfied the Reflexions caſt upon him in our Reſolutions of 
the 16th and 21ſt May laſt, were unjuft, unwarrantable, and without 
any Foundation whatſoever ; and do aſk Pardon of his Lordſhip and the 
Court of Common Pleas for the Indignity offered both to him and the 


Court, | 10th Noyember, 1746.

Although the Injury I have received might have required : a private 
Satisfaction, yet as the Offence was of a public Nature, and offered to 
the whole Court of Common Pleas, as well as myſelf, I thought i it more 
confiſtent with my Character, and the Dignity of the Poſt I have the Ho- 
nour to fill, to have Satisfaction in this public Manner ; and defire, 
with the Concurrence of my Brothers, that it may be regiſtered in. the 
Remembrance Office as a Memorial to the preſent and future Ages, that 
whoever ſec themſelves above the Law, will in the end find themſelves 
miſtaken ; for we may with propriety ay of the Law, as of Truth,
